# Excalidraw Data

## Text Elements
Key Concepts & Definitions Chapter 1-6 ^ksGkjRxs

Categorical: Data that represents groups or categories (e.g., eye color, gender, zip codes). ^dsMgWOQN

Quantitative: Numerical data that represents amounts or measurements (e.g., height, weight, test scores). ^JkcoRywX

Example: A dataset with students' favorite colors (categorical) and their heights (quantitative). ^RMYg0Fzh

Descriptive Statistics ^yEJnRz1E

Mean: Average value of a dataset. ^0MNlHZnX

Median: Middle value when data is ordered. ^OLxH4FMH

Mode: Most frequently occurring value. ^7ThL0BI9

Variance & Standard Deviation: Measure how spread out the data is. ^bmOmiXAt

Example: Given test scores [70, 80, 85, 90, 95], the mean is 84, median is 85, and standard deviation shows how much scores deviate from the mean. ^sfbBhb9W

Distribution Shapes ^hKCfCrR6

Symmetric: Left and right sides mirror each other. ^xLvkt2tS

Skewed Left: Tail on the left (e.g., exam scores where most students score high but a few score very low). ^YdnDo421

Skewed Right: Tail on the right (e.g., income distribution where most people earn lower amounts but a few earn very high). ^eCy8LZEb

Example: A histogram showing salaries where most employees earn $50,000 but a few executives earn $500,000 (right-skewed). ^mgg1ugXJ

Boxplots & Five-Number Summary ^VbQgRX0B

Five-Number Summary: 
 ^H3EXW6yl

Boxplot: A visual representation of the five-number summary. ^4R6r4odz

Normal Distribution ^I0i0dMPi

Bell-shaped, symmetric curve where most values cluster around the mean. ^CqUusZmr

68-95-99.7 Rule: 68% of data falls within 1 standard deviation, 95% within 2, and 99.7% within 3. ^vUGMsRB0

Z-score ^VO63IfK9

Measures how many standard deviations a value is from the mean. ^h8aBBe87

Formula:  ^YZPUbnF1

Example: If SAT scores have a mean of 500 and standard deviation of 100, a score of 650 has a z-score of (650-500)/100 = 1.5. ^eJrANH2q

Correlation (r) ^scBrbTEM

Measures strength & direction of a linear relationship between two variables (-1 to 1). ^HMDEMa1k

Example: Height and weight have a strong positive correlation (~0.8), meaning taller people tend to weigh more. ^rFP6Uule

Regression Equation ^CZvY74ci

Describes the relationship between dependent (Y) and independent (X) variables. ^I1hmrist

Formula:  ^pLCo96W5

Example: Predicting weight (Y) based on height (X), where y = 50 + 0.5x means for every 1 cm increase in height, weight increases by 0.5 kg. ^C9GVGWUS

Residuals ^k1XfHFGE

Difference between actual and predicted values in regression. ^KXULVHl7

Formula:  ^gaMplPgm

Example: If actual weight is 80 kg and predicted is 78 kg, residual is 80 - 78 = 2. ^rYN5uE7u

Relative Risk (RR) ^AI7iswWT

Compares risk between two groups. ^JZlUSolg

Formula: RR = P(disease | exposed) / P(disease | unexposed) ^joYP0Vwk

Example: Suppose we are studying lung cancer risk in smokers and non-smokers. The data is: ^HZcFm5Gg

Data Types ^FvOj83sj

Now, calculate the Relative Risk: RR = (0.10/0.02) = 5 This means that smokers are 5 times more likely to develop lung cancer compared to non-smokers. ^HUCiczHR

RR=1: No difference in risk between the two groups. ^8V4s6Sph

RR>1: Higher risk in the exposed group (risk factor). ^irNnL1tz

RR<1: Lower risk in the exposed group (protective factor). ^XwwFzQr1

Odds Ratio (OR) ^XXowYdkj

Compares odds of an event occurring in two groups. ^9k58U0es

Formula: OR = (p1 / (1 - p1)) / (p0 / (1 - p0)) ^xSADuH8j

Where: ^FdMaP5wP

p1​ = Probability of the event in the exposed group. ^dwNXokct

p0​ = Probability of the event in the unexposed group. ^OFhueO3q

1−p1​ and 1−p0​ are the probabilities of the event not occurring in the respective groups. ^0wAgrBTP

Example: If 20% of exposed people develop a disease while 5% of non-exposed do, OR = (0.2/0.8) / (0.05/0.95) = 4. ^iGZUCZ46

Standard Normal Table ^8igpzpgJ

Used to find probabilities for standard normal (Z) scores. ^LyVHHKyK

Example: If Z = 1.5, the table shows P(Z < 1.5) ≈ 0.933. ^k1dUJF5r

Important Formulas & Explanations ^MnpAn51W

Mean (x̄) ^JNrGGyvK

Formula: x̄ = Σxᵢ / n ^71c7ZNvc

Explanation: The sum of all observations divided by the number of observations. ^OOufkT51

Example: For the numbers [5, 10, 15], mean = (5+10+15)/3 = 10. ^xu7bC6o6

Variance (s²) ^dOhJ9zYq

Formula: s² = Σ (xᵢ - x̄)² / (n-1) ^wENmtirG

Explanation: Measures the spread of the data from the mean. ^tYLXvZe9

Example: If x̄ = 10 and data points are [5, 10, 15], variance = ((5-10)² + (10-10)² + (15-10)²) / (3-1) = 25. ^arajNnww

Standard Deviation (s) ^8bBIG8Sg

Formula: s = √s² ^5ep5jqhj

Explanation: The square root of variance gives the average deviation from the mean. ^S4PSQpRy

Correlation (r) ^e3GCikeh

Formula: r = Σ (xᵢ - x̄)(yᵢ - ȳ) / ( (n-1) * sₓ * sᵧ ) ^Va83FnSW

Explanation: Measures the strength and direction of a linear relationship between two variables. ^sgltmFaR

Example: If taller people tend to weigh more, r is positive. ^IFEQDNiX

Regression Slope (b) ^SW5kbm3H

Formula: b = r (sᵧ / sₓ) ^Am6DW6zU

Explanation: Represents the change in y for a one-unit increase in x. ^xA17cB9q

Example: If height increases by 1 cm, weight increases by 0.5 kg, b = 0.5. ^3k4VQhgk

Intercept (a) ^dYi9MQht

Formula: a = ȳ - b x̄ ^9DD0TUP1

Explanation: The predicted value of y when x = 0. ^Nb0Hfjlc

Example: If the regression equation is weight = 50 + 0.5(height), then when height = 0, predicted weight = 50. ^5Pxxb38f
